Cosmos Unveiled: Dark Matter Halos · Vol. 59

Galaxies are the bright minority of the universe. This book tells the story of the dark matter halos, the vast invisible spheres that outweigh their galaxies several times over, hold them together, and built them in the first place. It follows the century of evidence: Fritz Zwicky's fast-moving cluster galaxies in 1933, Vesto Slipher's first glimpse of Andromeda turning, Vera Rubin and Kent Ford's flat rotation curves that refuse to fall, and the modern maps made with gravitational lensing, X-ray gas, and shredded streams of stars. It explains how halos grew from the universe's first ripples,

History of Astronomy: Einstein's Revolution · Vol. 7

In 1905 a patent clerk removed absolute space and time from physics; in 1915 he replaced gravity with the shape of the universe itself. This is the story of Einstein's revolution, told as astronomy rather than biography: the cracks in Newton's confident universe, the two postulates that fused space with time, the curved geometry that made gravity a property of the landscape, and the observations that forced the world to believe it, from Mercury's drifting orbit to the eclipse of 1919 that bent starlight by two seconds of arc and made a theory famous. Earth's Sky: Noctilucent Clouds and Airglow · Vol. 17

The sky's two quietest lights live at the top of the atmosphere, and this book tells their whole story. Noctilucent clouds, first recorded in the twilight after Krakatoa and photographed and named by Otto Jesse in the 1880s, are rivers of silvery-blue ice at eighty-three kilometers, the highest weather on Earth, forming in the polar summer mesopause, the coldest place in the entire Earth system, on the smoke of meteors. Earth's Sky: Red Sky at Morning · Vol. 21

The oldest weather forecast still in daily use is a rhyme about the color of the sky, and this book takes it seriously: quoted in the Gospel of Matthew as wisdom already proverbial, red sky at morning is a working piece of meteorology, not folklore. The rhyme works because mid-latitude weather travels west to east: a gilded western sunset samples the dry, clean air of an approaching high, while a red dawn means clear air to the east and moisture and cloud coming from the west. Earth's Sky: The Seasons · Vol. 37

Why summer is warm and winter is cold, told honestly at last. The seasons are not caused by the Earth's distance from the Sun, in fact the Earth is FARTHEST from the Sun in July, but by the twenty-three-and-a-half-degree tilt of its axis, and this book is the biography of that lean. Earth's Sky: The Magnitude Scale · Vol. 50

Why do astronomers call the brightest stars by the smallest numbers? The magnitude scale is astronomy's oldest working instrument: a ranking of star brightness begun by Hipparchus in the second century BCE, made exact by Pogson's 1856 rule that each step means a fixed ratio, and stretched by modern photometry into a single ruler spanning everything from the noonday Sun to the faintest galaxies. From mile-thick ice sheets that once buried much of North America and Europe to the slow orbital rhythms that set them advancing and retreating, this book explores the coldest chapters in Earth's history. The EPR Paradox · Vol. 42

Can quantum mechanics be a complete description of reality? In 1935 Einstein, Podolsky, and Rosen argued no: if measuring one particle lets you predict another's properties with certainty at any distance, those properties must be real, yet quantum states cannot represent them all, so something is missing. This book traces the whole arc of the argument that refused to die: the Einstein-Bohr debates, Schrödinger's coining of entanglement, von Neumann's flawed impossibility proof, and John Bell's 1964 theorem, which turned a philosophical standoff into a measurable number. The Uncertainty Principle · Vol. 56

Why can nothing — no particle, no instrument, no trick — ever have both a perfectly defined position and a perfectly defined momentum? This book explains the uncertainty principle, the law Werner Heisenberg wrote down at twenty-five that turned measurement into bookkeeping: the product of the spreads of conjugate quantities is bounded by Planck's constant, a trade-off woven into states themselves rather than caused by clumsy instruments.
